Text:"Jeveration: System One Models Now Generate Yuhe Liu Qi Li Shihua Zhang Xinchao Wangyuhe liu@u.nus.edu liqi@u.nus.edu suhzhang001@gmail.com xinchao@nus.edu.sg Figure 1. System One models now generate. One frozen vision-language model regards text-to-image and image-to-image tasks as typed decisions. Text-to-image samples at 512 px (top) and image-conditioned generation and dense prediction (middle), all from the same Jeveration checkpoint. The bottom charts plot model size (a) and GenEval (b) against latency on one H200.System One models such as Jev return decisions through fast, parallel prediction. In this paper, we introduce Jeveration, showing that the same decision paradigm can also generate high-quality images in single forward pass. Jeveration builds on frozen vision-language model, repurpos- *Equal contribution Corresponding author ing its visual-semantic knowledge for holistic image generation. The key challenge is to maintain global consistency. single prompt admits many valid images, yet parallel predictions must converge on coherent whole. We therefore condition all positions on shared random state to coordinate parallel predictions, so that each draw yields different but coherent image. Since the model tends to ignore this state under direct image supervision, we first learn distribution over discrete image codes to guide single-pass gen1 eration. Beyond text-to-image generation, we further unify image-to-image generation and dense prediction within the same architecture, so that one checkpoint supports all three tasks, each in one forward pass. Experiments show that Jeveration generates 512512 image in 78 ms, up to 122 faster than existing unified models, while maintaining competitive image quality and dense prediction performance. 1. Introduction Image generation today is dominated by two paradigms. Autoregressive models [11, 40, 44] generate an image token by token, while diffusion models [8, 25, 48] generate it by iterative denoising. Both generally require many sequential model evaluations for single image, which increases inference latency. We introduce third paradigm for image generation that removes sequential sampling, built on System One models. Our model, Jeveration, returns complete image as one joint decision in single forward pass, given text prompt or source image  (Fig. 1)  . System One models such as Jev [1] return typed decisions, such as Choices or Scores, in single parallel query without extended reasoning [1]. They have been applied to option selection, object localization, robot action scoring, and image evaluation [12, 26, 27, 42, 43]. Their effectiveness in these decision-making and understanding tasks draws in part on the visual and semantic knowledge acquired through large-scale pretraining. This knowledge also offers foundation for image synthesis, suggesting that System One models have generative potential beyond their current uses. Motivated by this potential, we first explore direct approach: using System One model to select an entry from an existing codebook for every code position in single forward pass. Our initial attempts, however, reveal two key challenges. First, direct parallel selection does not ensure global coherence. single prompt admits many valid images, thus parallel decisions at different positions may correspond to incompatible realizations, producing image regions that fail to fit together, as illustrated in Fig. 2. Second, extending this approach to diverse image generation tasks requires unified architecture that accommodates different inputs and conditioning requirements. To keep the image globally consistent, we condition all positions on shared random state that coordinates the parallel predictions, so that each draw yields different but coherent image. Furthermore, since model trained on target images alone tends to ignore this state, we first train model that learns the distribution of images as discrete codes, and then use it to judge the single-pass decisions and guide their training. The same paradigm extends to further tasks whose output is an image. Image-to-image generation uses source image or visual conditions, such as edges, depth or grayscale, to guide the content and appearance Figure 2. Direct parallel selection yields incoherent images. Codes sampled independently from the first-phase model do not fit together, while Jeveration produces coherent images. of the generated image. Dense prediction can be regarded as image-to-image generation in broad sense, where the model generates map aligned with the input image that specifies class, depth or surface normal at each pixel. One model can thus cover generation as well as perception. Putting these together, Jeveration starts from visionlanguage model (VLM), Qwen3.5-4B [41], and lets it answer visual tasks with Jev-style decisions. The VLM stays frozen, and Jeveration adds only LoRA adapters [21] and output heads. Each answer, whether an image or dense map, is one typed decision made in single forward pass, and one checkpoint covers text-to-image generation, imageto-image generation and dense prediction tasks. It reaches 0.74 GenEval at 512 px in 78 ms per image on one H200, 122 faster than Janus-Pro-7B [11], closely follows the input structure in image-to-image generation, and achieves competitive results on dense prediction. These results show that decision model itself can generate images and predict dense maps in single forward pass. Our contributions are: Image generation through typed decisions. To our knowledge, this is the first work to show that Jev-style system one models can themselves generate images in one forward pass, for both text-to-image and image-toimage generation. Learning coherent image decisions. We let parallel decisions form one coherent image by conditioning them on one shared random state, and train the model to use this state by first learning the image distribution and then using it to judge and guide the decisions. One checkpoint across visual outputs. One checkpoint with shared adapters performs text-to-image generation, image-to-image generation and dense prediction. Extensive experiments and ablation studies on these tasks demonstrate its effectiveness and efficiency. 2. Related work Unified multimodal understanding and generation. Unified multimodal models combine image understanding and synthesis. Chameleon [40], Emu3 [44], Janus/JanusPro [11, 45] and Unified-IO 2 [33] generate image tokens Show-o [49] uses masked predic"

Abstract Games are rich testbed for visual agents, demanding perception, long-horizon planning, and precise control. However, collecting human gameplay is costly, and learning from gameplay videos requires inferring missing actions. Existing datasets also cover limited range of games, often without explicit tasks or verifiable outcomes. To address these gaps, we introduce Game2Player, framework that co-develops game environments and players to collect diverse and verifiable gameplay experience at scale. In the Game2Player loop, Builder agent adapts existing web games, exposes their internal state, and defines tasks with state-based progress measures and success checks. Coding Player and VLM Player then attempt the tasks and report problems to the Builder for repair, so that play serves as both environment validation and training data collection. With Game2Player, we build 568 verified web games with 3,436 tasks, collect 15.7K trajectories (307 hours of gameplay), and hold out 50 games as the benchmark PlayerBench. To evaluate the collected experience as training data, we train VLM agents with progress-weighted SFT, assigning greater weight to steps that advance task progress. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing userdefined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. Text:"LightTAR: Light and Fast Target-Aware Representations for Multimodal Tabular Learning Eilam Shapira 1 Alan Arazi 1 Shoham Grunblat 1 Roi ReichartMultimodal tabular learning involves classification and regression on tables combining structured numerical and categorical features with unstructured text and images. These unstructured fields are commonly represented by pretrained encoder embeddings reduced with principal component analysis (PCA). This reduction prioritizes input variance but may overlook information relevant to predicting the target. We introduce LightTAR, simple, inexpensive method that adapts dimensionality reduction to the prediction task. LightTAR augments the standard PCA representation with few target-aware features extracted jointly from the final and an intermediate encoder layer using regularized partial least squares (PLS). We evaluate LightTAR with 21 tabular models, including tree ensembles, neural networks, and tabular foundation models. LightTAR improves average predictive performance for all models and, averaged across models, improves prediction on almost every MulTaBench dataset. These gains come with small increase in total runtime relative to PCA representations. LightTAR integrates directly into existing encoding pipelines, improving tabular learners predictive performance without modifying the encoder or the learner.1 1. Introduction Tabular foundation models (TFMs) have recently established the state of the art in tabular learning (Erickson et al., 2025), learning generalizable representations of numerical and categorical data through pretraining (Hollmann et al., 2025; Qu et al., 2026a; Ma et al., 2025). Real tables, however, often carry free text and images alongside their numerical and categorical columns. For example, dermatology 1Technion Israel Institute of Technology. Correspondence to: Eilam Shapira <eilam.shapira@gmail.com>. Preprint. November 16, 2026. 1Code is available at https://github.com/eilamshapira/LightTAR. 1 record might combine patients age, photo of lesion, and the clinicians notes, while used-car listing might pair its price and model year with photos of the car and the sellers description. Predicting from such tables is known as Multimodal Tabular Learning (MMTL) (Huang et al., 2020; Arazi et al., 2026). While numerical and categorical features are consumed by tabular learner directly, text and images must first be encoded. MMTL pipelines usually do this with pretrained embedding model. This approach has two main limitations. First, the resulting representations are general-purpose and not adapted to the prediction tasks labels. Since pretrained encoders are trained to serve general-purpose objectives, their representations encode diverse information, some of which might be irrelevant to the task at hand. Second, these representations are typically high-dimensional, making them computationally costly for tabular learners. In particular, pretrained encoder typically represents text or images as vector of several hundred dimensions, so single column can contribute more features than the rest of the table holds. gradient-boosted ensemble evaluates candidate features at every split, so its training cost grows with the number of columns. With current tabular foundation models, the problem can be even more severe: they struggle with large feature counts and, in some cases, cannot handle them at all (Hollmann et al., 2025). To address the high dimensionality of these representations, conventional MMTL pipelines reduce the embeddings before the learner sees them (Grinsztajn et al., 2023; Blayer et al., 2026). Conventionally, this reduction is unsupervised and, as such, is not adapted to the prediction task. Each text or image value is encoded using pretrained encoder, and PCA is then fitted over the embeddings of the columns training examples. Only small number of components explaining most of the input variance are retained. We call this conventional approach the unsupervised pipeline. Its features can be handed to wide range of tabular learners, including tree ensembles such as XGBoost (Chen & Guestrin, 2016) and CatBoost (Prokhorenkova et al., 2018), neural tabular architectures such as RealMLP (Holzmuller et al., 2024) and TabM (Gorishniy et al., 2025), and tabular foundation models such as TabPFN (Hollmann et al., 2025), LightTAR: Light and Fast Target-Aware Representations for Multimodal Tabular Learning Figure 1. The Illustrated LightTAR Algorithm. frozen embedding model encodes the text or image column. PCA reduces its final-layer representation to features, while regularized PLS uses training labels during fitting to extract target-aware features from the concatenated final and intermediate layers. TabICLv2 (Qu et al., 2026a), and Kumo-Tabular (Qu et al., 2026b). Although PCA reduces the dimensionality of the representations, it selects projection components based on input variance, without considering their relationship to the target (Jolliffe, 1982). As result, information important for prediction may be discarded during dimensionality reduction. Since usually only few dozen components are kept, direction explaining little input variance may be discarded even when it contains information highly correlated with the task labels, such as rare phrase in text or small visual distinction. Using the task labels to select the projection directions instead yields Target-Aware Representation (TAR). Arazi et al. (2025; 2026) obtain one by fine-tuning the encoders against the task labels, improving the performance of predictive models on considerable share of the datasets in common multimodal benchmarks. However, fine-tuning an encoder remains slow and costly, and still risks overfitting, even when the model is small and only few layers are adapted using LoRA (Hu et al., 2022). Beyond how the dimensionality of the representations is reduced, the encoder layers from which information is extracted also matter. In the conventional pipeline, the representation of text or image column is typically taken from the encoders final layer. This layer is trained to place inputs that share meaning close together, whether through contrastive objectives over text pairs (Reimers & Gurevych, 2019; Wang et al., 2024) or through invariance to augmented views of an image (Simeoni et al., 2025). Such objectives encourage the encoder to retain information relevant to its pretraining objectives, but not necessarily the distinctions needed for the tabula"

However, unlike datacenter servers with abundant DRAM, consumer devices are tightly constrained in both GPU VRAM and host RAM, forcing massive MoE weights to spill across VRAM, host RAM, and SSD. Under such spilling, we observe that existing systems fail to fully utilize this memory hierarchy, treating the tiers as independent caches and thrashing in inference. We present TIDEWAY, system for efficient inference of large MoE models on memory-constrained consumer devices. Our core insight is to manage host RAM and GPU VRAM as unified memory pool. Within this pool, TIDEWAY couples phase-aware memory layouts, which adapt to the distinct access patterns of prefill and decode, with an execution engine that optimizes CUDA Graph execution under expert paging. Our evaluations on representative consumer hardware with frontier MoE models demonstrate that TIDEWAY significantly outperforms state-of-the-art systems. On average, TIDEWAY achieves speedups of 1.97 in end-to-end latency, 2.40 in Time-to-First-Token (TTFT), and 1.61 in Time-Per-Output-Token (TPOT) against the fastest baseline. The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories.1 Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and shared research memory connect these actions across the model familys development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI. Text:"QDA: CLOSED-LOOP LLM QUANTIZATION DESIGN WITH AGENTS Xingyang Li Tianlang Zhao Song Han Ligeng Zhu ABSTRACT Post-training quantization is the standard way to serve large language models at low cost. However, the best quantization recipe differs across models and precisions. As result, experts must redesign it for every new model, which is time-consuming and often sub-optimal. Fortunately, an LLM agent can take over this design by refining the recipe over rounds of building and evaluating candidates, so that more rounds lead to better recipes. However, evaluating one quantized reasoning model takes tens of GPU-hours, and combining methods from different codebases means porting each to the new architecture by hand. We present QDA, system in which an agent designs the recipe for each model and precision. To make evaluation fast, QDA scores each candidate in minutes by its output KL divergence from the original model on reasoning traces that the agent never sees, and checks promising ones with long generations to catch failures such as repetition loops. To make building fast, QDA provides library of public PTQ methods that are compatible with one another and with new model architectures. Across four reasoning models, QDA improves accuracy over the strongest public recipe by 0.9, 1.4 and 2.2 percentage points on average at W4A16, W4A8 and W3A16, using 64 GPU-hours per search. On the 320B-parameter MoE model GLM-5.3-Flash at NVFP4 W4A4, it recovers 98.3% of the FP8 models accuracy, against 96.9% for the best vendor checkpoint. Every QDA checkpoint also runs on existing serving kernels with no inference overhead. We will release the library, the evaluator and the harness. Large language models (LLMs) excel at mathematics, science and programming, and reasoning models go further by thinking for thousands of tokens before they answer (Guo et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2025a; Chen et al., 2026). However, serving them is costly in both memory and compute. Posttraining quantizatio"

Text:"QDA: CLOSED-LOOP LLM QUANTIZATION DESIGN WITH AGENTS Xingyang Li Tianlang Zhao Song Han Ligeng ZhuABSTRACT Post-training quantization is the standard way to serve large language models at low cost. However, the best quantization recipe differs across models and precisions. As result, experts must redesign it for every new model, which is time-consuming and often sub-optimal. Fortunately, an LLM agent can take over this design by refining the recipe over rounds of building and evaluating candidates, so that more rounds lead to better recipes. However, evaluating one quantized reasoning model takes tens of GPU-hours, and combining methods from different codebases means porting each to the new architecture by hand. We present QDA, system in which an agent designs the recipe for each model and precision. To make evaluation fast, QDA scores each candidate in minutes by its output KL divergence from the original model on reasoning traces that the agent never sees, and checks promising ones with long generations to catch failures such as repetition loops. To make building fast, QDA provides library of public PTQ methods that are compatible with one another and with new model architectures. Across four reasoning models, QDA improves accuracy over the strongest public recipe by 0.9, 1.4 and 2.2 percentage points on average at W4A16, W4A8 and W3A16, using 64 GPU-hours per search. On the 320B-parameter MoE model GLM-5.3-Flash at NVFP4 W4A4, it recovers 98.3% of the FP8 models accuracy, against 96.9% for the best vendor checkpoint. Every QDA checkpoint also runs on existing serving kernels with no inference overhead. We will release the library, the evaluator and the harness.Large language models (LLMs) excel at mathematics, science and programming, and reasoning models go further by thinking for thousands of tokens before they answer (Guo et al., 2025; Yang et al., 2025a; Chen et al., 2026). However, serving them is costly in both memory and compute. Posttraining quantization (PTQ) is standard way to cut both costs (Frantar et al., 2023; Lin et al., 2024b). For example, with its weights in NVFP4, the 320B-parameter GLM-5.3Flash (Z.ai, 2026) fits on one GB300 GPU instead of the two that it needs in FP8. Quantizing the activations as well lets matrix multiplications run on low-precision tensor cores, which are several times faster than BF16 ones (Xiao et al., 2023; Lin et al., 2025; Alvarez et al., 2025). Existing PTQ methods fix different sources of quantization error (Xiao et al., 2023; Frantar et al., 2023; Lin et al., 2024b), so they can be stacked, and combination often works better than any single method (Ashkboos et al., 2024; Liu et al., 2025b). quantized checkpoint is therefore produced by recipe, which consists of the stacked methods, their settings and their calibration data ( 2.2). However, we find that the best recipe differs across models and precisions. For example, with 4-bit weight-only quantization, the most accurate public recipe is ParoQuant (Liang et al., 2026) on Equal contribution. Figure 1. QDA beats the strongest public recipe at low cost. Left: average accuracy of four reasoning models at each precision, for the strongest public recipe and for QDA. Middle: QDA checkpoints run on existing kernels with no inference overhead. Right: the GPU-hours and agent tokens of one search. Qwen3.5-9B (Qwen Team, 2026) but GPTQ (Frantar et al., 2023) on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (Figure 2(a)). On Qwen3.5-9B alone, AWQ (Lin et al., 2024b) beats GPTQ in 4-bit but loses to it in 3-bit weight-only quantization. Each method is evaluated on only few models and precisions, so the best recipe for new model can only be found by trying candidates on it. Experts therefore still decide by hand which methods to combine, with which settings and data. For example, NVIDIAs ModelOpt keeps separate hand-written recipe for each of 28 models, which records model-specific decisions such as which modules to leave in BF16 (NVIDIA, 2024). Such hand design does not scale, QDA: Closed-Loop LLM Quantization Design with Agents because every new model needs its own recipes and new models arrive quickly. Between October 2025 and September 2026, more than 90 open-weight models with over 10B parameters were released (Epoch AI, 2026), which would need over 360 recipes at our four precisions. LLM coding agents already write code to improve algorithms, GPU kernels and computer systems (Novikov et al., 2025; Cao et al., 2026; Cheng et al., 2025). Recipe design is natural task for such an agent, because it also proceeds by trial and error, building and evaluating one candidate after another as an expert would. However, two problems stand in the way. First, evaluating candidate is expensive. Because reasoning model writes thousands of tokens per answer and each problem must be sampled many times, full evaluation of one quantized checkpoint takes up to 86 GPU-hours ( 3.2). At this cost, search can try only few candidates, so wider search needs much cheaper proxy metric for accuracy. Second, building candidate is costly. Most PTQ methods are released as separate research code, so recipe that mixes them must be ported by hand to each new architecture, such as linear-attention hybrids (Yang et al., 2025b; Kimi Team et al., 2025) and fine-grained mixtures of experts (DeepSeek-AI et al., 2024; OpenAI et al., 2025). Even method that toolkit supports can be too slow for search ( 3.3). An agent that fills these gaps itself writes about 3k lines of quantization code per search and still ends with less accurate checkpoint ( 6.3). To this end, we present QDA, system in which an agent designs the quantization recipe for each model and precision (Figure 3). To address the first problem, we use the KL divergence of the candidates next-token distributions from the original models as the proxy metric, measured on reference traces, reasoning traces that the original model wrote. Because the original models distributions are cached, scoring candidate takes minutes, which is 350990 cheaper than full evaluation. The agent also generates full answers for few promising candidates to reveal rare failures such as repetition loops. To address the second problem, we implement 21 PTQ methods in one library behind common interface. Text:"Ziqi Jin1, Jiaxi Li2, Weiwen Xu3,, Aixin Sun1, Project: xalp.net/late-entry Code: github.com/Xalp/LateComer Models: hf.co/XalphinionsLooped language models deepen reasoning at compact parameter count by iterating shared layer stack 𝑇 times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final 𝐾 < 𝑇 passes over deep context cache. During autoregressive decoding, however, running all 𝑇 passes per generated token turns recurrence depth directly into sequential latency. When each new token is restricted to 𝐾 < 𝑇 serial passes, which passes should it execute? As shown in Figure 1, conventional early exit without cache completion (Schuster et al., 2022) runs the first 𝐾 passes and halts (Figure 1b), leaving past key-value (KV) caches truncated at shallow depth 𝐾 so all future tokens attend to immature context states. We explore the opposite choice, late entry, which skips initial passes and feeds each new tokens embedding directly into the final 𝐾 passes over deep context KV cache (Figure 1c). At first glance, skipping initial passes seems destined to fail: on off-the-shelf Ouro-1.4B and Ouro-2.6B (𝑇 = 4) (Zhu et al., 2025), feeding raw embeddings into late pass breaks predictions immediately because late passes expect normalized hidden states from earlier passes. Once lightweight fine-tuning aligns late passes to accept raw embeddings, however, clear asymmetry emerges between token depth and context depth: one final pass over four-pass context matches full-depth predictions more closely than two initial passes over two-pass context (+10+ accuracy points in sequential decoding). Under identical training data, hyperparameters, and steps, spending 𝐾 serial passes at the end of the recurrence consistently outperforms spending them at the beginning across both Ouro scales (𝑇 = 4; Section 2) and 32-pass middle-recurrent Huginn-3.5B (𝑇 = 32; Section 4.4), showing that deep cached context retains much of the benefit of recurrent depth. Turning this finding into wall-clock speedups requires building deep context states without stalling generation. In late-entry block decoding paired with Wake verification (Section 3), parameter-free candidate blocks (Liu et al., 2026a; Jin et al., 2026) execute only the final 𝐾 passes on the critical path to emit tentatively accepted frontier and provisional KV states, while earlier tentative tokens complete true passes 1 through 𝑇 as piggybacked batch rows at zero extra serial calls (using spare per-call batch capacity in memory-bound decoding rather than reducing FLOPs). Text:"BiCAT: Bimanual Coordination Action Tokenization Xuanwei Liu1 Guipu Cao1 Zhongyi Luo1 Jiafei Duan*1 1National University of Singapore *Corresponding Author Webpage: magiclab-nus.github.io/BiCAT Code: github.com/MAGICLAB-NUS/BiCAT Contact: duanj1@nus.edu.sg Abstract Autoregressive vision-language-action (VLA) models predict discrete action tokens with the next-token objective of their vision-language backbone, so the action tokenizer defines the interface between the policy and the robot. In bimanual manipulation, the two arms share repertoire of movements yet coordinate through complementary roles, and an action representation should capture both. However, most existing tokenizers encode both arms as single concatenated action vector, in which motion patterns common to the two arms are not shared and cross-arm dependencies remain entangled within each token. The policy must therefore learn both aspects implicitly. We propose Bimanual Coordination Action Tokenization (BiCAT), which shares one motion representation across arms while conditioning each arms tokens on its partner through symmetric cross-arm attention and joint decoder. The policy then predicts all tokens of each arm in parallel, generating the left arm first and the right arm conditioned on it. Across ten RoboTwin 2.0 tasks and six real-world tasks on three embodiments, BiCAT achieves the highest success among the compared tokenizers. In simulation, it improves mean success by 17.6 percentage points over the strongest of eight baselines, with the largest gains on tasks that require close coordination between the arms, while requiring only 40.5 ms per action chunk. These results suggest that the action tokenizer is natural place to model bimanual coordination. Figure 1 bimanual robot is two arms, not one arm with twice the joints. Left: existing tokenizers treat the two arms as single arm and mix the left and right arm in every token; BiCAT gives each arm its own token stream, aware of its partner. Right:"

Text:"Chorus: Shared N-gram Embeddings with Layer-wise Adaptation Language models can gain capacity at little computational cost from n-gram embedding tables: large tables of learned vectors for short sequences of consecutive tokens, which are looked up and added to the models input or to its intermediate layers. Recent designs commonly use separate n-gram embedding table for each layer, which makes the tables costly in memory and prevents layers from sharing what they learn. Sharing single table, however, gives every layer the same embedding and leaves no room for layer-wise preferences, and it performs poorly in practice. We propose Chorus, in which one set of n-gram embedding tables serves the whole network and every layer reads it in its own way. Each layer maps the refined embedding through its own projection layer, after rescaling its components by factors computed from its current hidden state. Chorus matches the quality of existing n-gram methods with 87% smaller tables: with 1.2B table parameters it predicts text better than FactorEngram and DeepSeeks Engram do with 9.6B. It outperforms Engram, TN-gram and FactorEngram in every setting where we compared them, across table sizes from 20M to 9.6B parameters. We also show that the recently proposed memory-efficient optimizer Ember works well with Chorus, saving 99.2% of table-optimizer memory compared with AdamW without degrading training performance. These results enable further scaling of n-gram embedding tables along the table-size axis by reducing memory use and allowing shared knowledge to be reused across layers. Code: https://github.com/dualverse-ai/chorous Date: 18-Nov-2026 Correspondence: info@dualverse.ai In standard language model, model size and computation grow together because almost every weight takes part in processing every token [13]. N-gram embedding tables separate the two by adding large tables of learned vectors for short sequences of consecutive tokens. At each position, the model looks up vec"

Text:"PIPETTE: Prompt Injection Purple-Teaming for Robust LLM Agents Lipeng He, Yihan Wang(cid:66), Jiawen Zhang, N. Asokan University of Waterloo, Zhejiang University, KTH Royal Institute of Technology lipeng.he@uwaterloo.ca, yihan.wang@uwaterloo.ca, kevinzh@zju.edu.cn, asokan@acm.org Abstract Large language model (LLM) agents routinely interact with untrusted external data, exposing them to indirect prompt injection (IPI) attacks that hijack agents into executing attacker-chosen tasks. To uncover these vulnerabilities at scale, automated red-teaming methods often leverage reinforcement learning (RL) to generate attacks that achieve high attack success rates (ASR). Model developers use the attacks discovered during internal red-teaming to harden agents through adversarial training before deployment, collaborative offensive-defensive process known in cybersecurity as purple-teaming. However, we show that achieving high ASR during red-teaming does not guarantee robustness gains from subsequent adversarial training. Specifically, we find that two state-of-the-art RL-based red-teaming methods, RL-Hammer and PISmith, suffer from mode collapse, repeatedly discovering similar attack strategies whose additional successes add no additional robustness improvements to adversarial training. To address this gap, we introduce PIPETTE, unified prompt injection purple-teaming framework that effectively converts red-teaming discoveries into defender robustness improvements. PIPETTE employs dictionary learning-based reward that encourages broader coverage of attack strategies during redteaming. It also integrates an on-policy adversarial training pipeline tailored to IPI defense, enabling agents to resist attacks while completing legitimate user tasks. Extensive evaluations across agentic benchmarks (AgentDojo and ASB) demonstrate that PIPETTE produces defenders that achieve near-zero attack success rates on static attacks and can withstand strong adaptive attacks, while maintaining benig"

Text:"AVTR: Adaptive Multimodal Reasoning with Visual Imagination Xiaoqian Shen1,2,, Yunyang Xiong1,, Changsheng Zhao1, Saksham Suri1, Chong Zhou1, Zechun Liu1, Zhipeng Cai1, Zhuang Liu3, Qi Qian1, Wei Wen1, Hongyu Xu1, Hanxian Huang1, Ernie Chang1, Yanbei Chen1, Vikas Chandra1, Raghuraman Krishnamoorthi1, Mohamed Elhoseiny2,, Lemeng Wu1, 1Meta AI, 2King Abdullah University of Science and Technology (KAUST), 3Princeton University Work done at Meta, Project lead Vision-Language Models increasingly rely on textual reasoning to solve advanced multimodal tasks. Despite its effectiveness, this paradigm faces two fundamental limitations. First, relying on textual reasoning alone can limit perceptual ability in vision-centric questions, where accurate answers depend on interpreting task-relevant visual evidence. Second, applying textual reasoning uniformly across inputs overlooks question-dependent variation in both reasoning modality and depth, often producing verbose and unnecessary traces when little logical inference is required. To this end, we propose Adaptive Visual-Textual Reasoning (AVTR), which introduces visual imagination as complementary reasoning modality to textual thinking, enabling the model to revisit its visual observations and distill the salient evidence through generative visual reflection. Furthermore, we formulate reasoningmodality selection as learned routing problem, allowing single policy to adaptively harness the complementary strengths of visual reflection and textual reasoning in response to the perceptual and inferential demands of the input. Across 16 benchmarks, AVTR improves the average score by 4.5 points over Qwen3.5 baseline, while reducing inference latency by 33%. It also scales effectively into smaller size backbone, outperforming the evaluated 2B-scale baselines. Abstract Jeveration repurposes a frozen vision-language model to generate coherent images and perform image-to-image and dense prediction tasks in a single forward pass with fast 512x512 generation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash System One models such as Jev return decisions through fast, parallel prediction. In this paper, we introduce Jeveration, showing that the same decision paradigm can also generate high-quality images in a single forward pass. Jeveration builds on a frozen vision-language model, repurposing its visual-semantic knowledge for holistic image generation. The key challenge is to maintain global consistency. A single prompt admits many valid images, yet parallel predictions must converge on a coherent whole. We therefore condition all positions on a shared random state to coordinate parallel predictions, so that each draw yields a different but coherent image. Since the model tends to ignore this state under direct image supervision, we first learn a distribution over discrete image codes to guide single-pass generation. Beyond text-to-image generation, we further unify image-to-image generation and dense prediction within the same architecture, so that one checkpoint supports all three tasks, each in one forward pass. Experiments show that Jeveration generates a 512x512 image in 78 ms, up to 122x faster than existing unified models, while maintaining competitive image quality and dense prediction performance.
[19.11.2026 11:07] Response: {"desc": "Авторы предлагают Jeveration — метод, который адаптирует замороженную vision-language модель для генерации изображений за один проход вперёд (single forward pass). Ключевая сложность в том, что параллельные предсказания должны согласованно складываться в целое изображение, поэтому все позиции обусловливаются общим случайным состоянием, а распределение над дискретными image codes обучается для направления генерации. Одна и та же архитектура и один checkpoint также решают задачи image-to-image и dense prediction. Модель генерирует изображение 512x512 за 78 мс, что до 122 раз быстрее существующих unified моделей при сопоставимом качестве.", "emoji": "⚡", "title": "Картинка за один проход: быстрая и согласованная генерация изображений"}

Abstract  High-resolution weather prediction is important for resolving local atmospheric patterns missed by coarse global forecasts. Large pretrained weather backbones have improved global forecasting, but adapting them to produce future high-resolution fields from recent coarse states remains challenging and costly. We formulate this setting as Predictive Spatiotemporal Weather Downscaling (PSWD), a task that jointly refines spatial and temporal resolution while predicting future high-resolution trajectories. To this end, we propose a framework that decomposes prediction into spatial and temporal stages using a shared pretrained backbone, fixed numerical scaffolds, and lightweight residual heads. For backbone adaptation, we introduce PROLA (PRincipal-Orthogonal Low-rank Adaptation), which allocates a fixed low-rank budget between pretrained weights' principal directions and their orthogonal complement. PROLA further rescales rank-1 optimizer updates using gradient signals, improving adaptation without increasing trainable rank. Experiments in the PSWD setting show that PROLA outperforms representative low-rank adaptation baselines. Further experiments across diverse datasets and tasks support the broader applicability of PROLA, showing that its benefits extend beyond the primary weather downscaling setting.

Авторы предлагают разделять предсказание на пространственный и временной этапы с использованием общего предобученного backbone, фиксированных численных каркасов и легковесных residual heads. Для адаптации backbone вводится метод PROLA, который распределяет фиксированный бюджет low-rank адаптации между главными направлениями весов и их ортогональным дополнением. Abstract  Games are a rich testbed for visual agents, demanding perception, long-horizon planning, and precise control. However, collecting human gameplay is costly, and learning from gameplay videos requires inferring missing actions. Existing datasets also cover a limited range of games, often without explicit tasks or verifiable outcomes. To address these gaps, we introduce Game2Player, a framework that co-develops game environments and players to collect diverse and verifiable gameplay experience at scale. In the Game2Player loop, a Builder agent adapts existing web games, exposes their internal state, and defines tasks with state-based progress measures and success checks. A Coding Player and a VLM Player then attempt the tasks and report problems to the Builder for repair, so that play serves as both environment validation and training data collection. With Game2Player, we build 568 verified web games with 3,436 tasks, collect 15.7K trajectories (307 hours of gameplay), and hold out 50 games as the benchmark PlayerBench. To evaluate the collected experience as training data, we train VLM agents with progress-weighted SFT, assigning greater weight to steps that advance task progress. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract  Games are a rich testbed for visual agents, demanding perception, long-horizon planning, and precise control. However, collecting human gameplay is costly, and learning from gameplay videos requires inferring missing actions. Existing datasets also cover a limited range of games, often without explicit tasks or verifiable outcomes. To address these gaps, we introduce Game2Player, a framework that co-develops game environments and players to collect diverse and verifiable gameplay experience at scale. In the Game2Player loop, a Builder agent adapts existing web games, exposes their internal state, and defines tasks with state-based progress measures and success checks. A Coding Player and a VLM Player then attempt the tasks and report problems to the Builder for repair, so that play serves as both environment validation and training data collection. With Game2Player, we build 568 verified web games with 3,436 tasks, collect 15.7K trajectories (307 hours of gameplay), and hold out 50 games as the benchmark PlayerBench. To evaluate the collected experience as training data, we train VLM agents with progress-weighted SFT, assigning greater weight to steps that advance task progress. Game2Player-9B improves progress from 14.3% to 31.2% on PlayerBench and from 30.0% to 53.1% on the external GameWorld benchmark. More broadly, our work suggests a path for visual agents to scale their gameplay experience by building, verifying, and learning from existing game worlds."

Abstract Agent evolution is often bottlenecked by noisy evaluations that cannot reliably identify better candidate artifacts or rank methods, so progress requires making true improvements detectable and accumulable.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Agent evolution follows a simple loop: a proposer writes candidate artifacts (prompts, skills, memories) and selection accepts those that score higher. This loop has shown promise, yet practitioners face a puzzle: methods trade wins across papers, and evolution often fails to bootstrap on certain tasks. We trace this puzzle to the Resolution Hypothesis: agent evolution stalls not because methods fail to propose better artifacts, but because noisy evaluations cannot reliably identify them. Across seven evolution methods and six models (over 10M LLM calls), we find support for the hypothesis at both levels where evaluation scores are used. At the candidate level (choosing artifacts), helpful candidate artifacts exist, but selection is no better than random at telling them from harmful ones, so errors accumulate across rounds. At the method level (ranking methods), typical margins of a few percentage points are smaller than the difference between two independent runs of the same method, so published rankings often rest on luck. Together, these findings indicate that agent evolution is often bottlenecked by reliably identifying improvements. This calls for a shift in how we develop and evaluate self-improving agents, from designing ever more candidate improvement systems to making true improvements detectable and accumulable.
[19.11.2026 11:08] Response: {"desc": "Авторы утверждают, что эволюция LLM-агентов упирается не в умение генерировать улучшения, а в шумные оценки, которые не позволяют надёжно отличить хорошие артефакты от плохих. Гипотеза разрешения проверяется на семи методах и шести моделях (более 10 млн вызовов LLM) на двух уровнях. На уровне кандидатов выбор артефактов не лучше случайного, и ошибки накапливаются от раунда к раунду. На уровне методов различия в пару процентов меньше разброса между повторными запусками одного метода, поэтому опубликованные рейтинги часто основаны на удаче. At the candidate level (choosing artifacts), helpful candidate artifacts exist, but selection is no better than random at telling them from harmful ones, so errors accumulate across rounds. At the method level (ranking methods), typical margins of a few percentage points are smaller than the difference between two independent runs of the same method, so published rankings often rest on luck. Together, these findings indicate that agent evolution is often bottlenecked by reliably identifying improvements. This calls for a shift in how we develop and evaluate self-improving agents, from designing ever more candidate improvement systems to making true improvements detectable and accumulable."

Abstract CurvDelta and CurvLoRA constrain LLM updates to a low-curvature preservation space, improving retention while maintaining target adaptation across reasoning, code, and unlearning tasks.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash A central challenge in adapting large language models (LLMs) is capability preservation: an adaptation can achieve its target while degrading general capabilities. We introduce CurvDelta, a scalable framework that incorporates the preservation constraint into the parameterization of the model update. By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. Both methods apply to preservation-constrained adaptation tasks such as capability learning and selective unlearning. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. For knowledge unlearning, our main results on TOFU and MUSE show a better balance between targeted forgetting and knowledge retention than the baselines, with retention performance close to the from-scratch retraining reference.

Для практичности используются K-FAC аппроксимация кривизны и факторизованная проекция. Методы тестируются на математическом рассуждении, генерации кода и selective unlearning (TOFU, MUSE). Abstract A rule-graph-based generative world model preserves user-defined rules and world states across saving and loading while accurately realizing rule-induced visual changes on designated objects and attributes.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The former requires preserving rule definitions, dependencies, and world states across saving and loading, so that restored worlds follow the established rules. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.
[19.11.2026 11:08] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен WorldRuler — генеративная модель мира на основе графа правил, которая сохраняет пользовательские правила и состояния мира при сохранении и загрузке. Граф правил (3D rule graph) хранит определения правил, зависимости и состояния объектов и связывает исполнение правил, генерацию видео через LLM-подобные и diffusion-модели и перцептивную обратную связь в замкнутый цикл. Динамическое связывание объектов (dynamic object binding) привязывает сущности из правил к визуальным экземплярам и ограничивает генерацию сохранёнными состояниями даже до их первого появления в кадре. Для обучения авторы вводят замену состояний атрибутов объектов и counterfactual reconstruction, а также датасет RuleVerse, собранный автоматически с помощью GTA-V и Habitat, с подробными аннотациями состояний и событий.", "emoji": "🎮", "title": "Правила игры, которые не теряются при сохранении"}

Abstract  World action models (WAMs) have shown promising generalization and data efficiency in robot manipulation by coupling action generation with predictions of future visual dynamics. Translating these capabilities into reliable task execution, however, remains a challenge for existing post-training methods. Offline supervised fine-tuning provides dense supervision on expert trajectories, but its flow-matching action expert is trained only on noised demonstration actions and never on the intermediate states produced by its own sampler; task-specific adaptation can also compromise pretrained capabilities. On-policy reinforcement learning trains on the policy's own behavior, yet sparse task rewards make exploration and credit assignment difficult, while dense reward shaping still provides scalar evaluations rather than direct action targets. We introduce HINT-WAM, the first on-policy self-distillation framework that supervises the action expert on its own sampling trajectories using hindsight, the future video of the demonstration, as privileged information available to the WAM itself. For each demonstration observation, the student generates its action through its own denoising trajectory, and at the states along this trajectory the same model acts as a teacher conditioned additionally on this hindsight, providing a conditional action distribution as a dense training target, while the student sees only deployment-time inputs and needs no environmental rewards or simulator interaction. To transfer this supervision to a flow-matching action expert, HINT-WAM distills the teacher into the student with an energy score, a distribution-level objective that matches the teacher's action distribution from the student's own samples rather than regressing its velocity field. The project web is available in https://tuandattt.github.io/hintwam/.

Учитель даёт плотный сигнал в виде условного распределения действий, без наград среды и симулятора. Abstract LightTAR adapts dimensionality reduction to the prediction task by adding target-aware features from encoder layers via regularized partial least squares, improving multimodal tabular model performance with minimal runtime overhead.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal tabular learning involves classification and regression on tables combining structured numerical and categorical features with unstructured text and images. These unstructured fields are commonly represented by pretrained encoder embeddings reduced with principal component analysis (PCA). This reduction prioritizes input variance but may overlook information relevant to predicting the target. We introduce LightTAR, a simple, inexpensive method that adapts dimensionality reduction to the prediction task. LightTAR augments the standard PCA representation with a few target-aware features extracted jointly from the final and an intermediate encoder layer using regularized partial least squares (PLS). We evaluate LightTAR with 21 tabular models, including tree ensembles, neural networks, and tabular foundation models. LightTAR improves average predictive performance for all models and, averaged across models, improves prediction on almost every MulTaBench dataset. These gains come with a small increase in total runtime relative to PCA representations. LightTAR integrates directly into existing encoding pipelines, improving tabular learners' predictive performance without modifying the encoder or the learner.

Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Multimodal tabular learning involves classification and regression on tables combining structured numerical and categorical features with unstructured text and images. LightTAR integrates directly into existing encoding pipelines, improving tabular learners' predictive performance without modifying the encoder or the learner."

Abstract  Visual proxies decouple explicit world-state simulation from photorealistic video synthesis, offering a controllable interface for both offline clip creation and real-time interactive streaming. Yet a practical renderer must address two challenges. Upstream proxies vary from coarse layouts to fine articulated geometry depending on the interaction, and streaming inference must operate in a few causal steps without accumulating drift across self-generated history. We present Invoker-Render, pairing a bidirectional creation model with a causal four-step streaming model under a shared conditioning interface over structural proxies, an appearance reference, and language. Trained on an aligned approximately 200-hour multi-granularity corpus, Invoker-Render honors fine geometric constraints when supplied while synthesizing plausible local motion under coarse proxies, enabling runtime selection of input detail. To bridge bidirectional creation and real-time streaming without compounding step compression and rollout drift, we introduce Progressive Forcing (ProForcing). ProForcing first trains a causal predictor and distills it into a four-step renderer using histories derived from training videos. Training then shifts to continuous self-generated rollouts. A pipelined serving stack supports efficient streaming inference. Experiments show versatile control across proxy families, granularities, and camera paths, consistent gains over recent streaming baselines, and stable 30-minute racing rollouts that preserve scene and subject appearance.

[19.11.2026 11:08] OpenRouter request. Model: anthropic/claude-haiku-5.5. Abstract  NPU design-space exploration for LLM inference mainly relies on an expensive simulator that often returns only scalar feedback such as latency and power. Such feedback can rank evaluated configurations, but gives little information for deciding why a configuration is slow or which architecture parameter should be changed next. This limitation is especially severe because prefill and decode workloads can be limited by different resources, including compute throughput and HBM bandwidth. We propose CriticNPU, a sample-efficient DSE framework built around an online-calibrated mechanistic critic for NPU architecture design. The critic uses explicit hardware limits to evaluate NPU configurations: before simulation, it estimates whether a proposed configuration is likely to improve over the current best and filters weak candidates; after simulation, it aggregates operator-level runtimes into prefill/decode bottleneck summaries and predicts the effect of candidate parameter changes. We instantiate the critic with a Roofline-style model that derives compute and memory limits from architecture parameters and calibrates per-operator-type effective limits using simulator observations. And CriticNPU combines this critic with a workload extractor and an architecture planner to propose configurations. Across six LLM workloads and 16 deployment configurations per model, CriticNPU achieves best-or-tied-best latency in 74% of prefill and 87.5% of decode settings while using roughly half the simulator calls of competitive DSE baselines. We conduct extensive ablation studies and detailed analyses to validate the effectiveness of our method from multiple perspectives. Our code is available at https://github.com/bruno686/CriticNPU.

Ключевая идея — механистический критик на основе Roofline-модели, который использует явные аппаратные ограничения по вычислениям и пропускной способности HBM и калибруется онлайн по наблюдениям симулятора для каждого типа операторов. Abstract A method that removes the decoder from latent diffusion models by adding a final-step prediction head to a finetuned diffusion transformer, reducing inference cost and improving pixel quality in image and video generation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Latent diffusion models rely on a decoder to map denoised latents to pixels, adding complexity and inference cost. We introduce Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), which lets a finetuned diffusion transformer produce final pixels directly through a linear head. Unlike previous methods, where the denoising output leads to final pixels, DWD separates the two roles: the original denoising head continues to drive sampling, then the new DWD head predicts the pixels at the final step. We extend pretrained latent models with DWD efficiently by finetuning only the last few transformer blocks, training the DWD head with pixel quality losses while anchoring the denoising head to the pretrained model to preserve the sample trajectory. Across latent image and video generators, DWD removes the decoder and reduces inference cost while maintaining or even improving pixel quality. On Wan video generation, it visibly reduces detail distortion and flickering, lowering warping error by 23%. Remarkably, the same separation also improves pixel diffusion models that do not even use a decoder: a final-step DWD head produces substantially better texture detail in text-to-image generation, and improves ImageNet FID from 1.55 to 1.43, a new state of the art among pixel flow models.
[19.11.2026 11:09] Response: {"desc": "Авторы предлагают метод Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), который убирает декодер из латентных диффузионных моделей. Дообученный diffusion transformer с помощью линейной головы напрямую предсказывает пиксели на финальном шаге. Исходная denoising-голова продолжает управлять сэмплированием, а новая DWD-голова обучается с pixel-quality лоссами, при этом траектория сэмплирования сохраняется за счёт привязки к предобученной модели. "Abstract A method that removes the decoder from latent diffusion models by adding a final-step prediction head to a finetuned diffusion transformer, reducing inference cost and improving pixel quality in image and video generation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Latent diffusion models rely on a decoder to map denoised latents to pixels, adding complexity and inference cost. We introduce Decoding Without a Decoder (DWD), which lets a finetuned diffusion transformer produce final pixels directly through a linear head. Unlike previous methods, where the denoising output leads to final pixels, DWD separates the two roles: the original denoising head continues to drive sampling, then the new DWD head predicts the pixels at the final step. We extend pretrained latent models with DWD efficiently by finetuning only the last few transformer blocks, training the DWD head with pixel quality losses while anchoring the denoising head to the pretrained model to preserve the sample trajectory. Across latent image and video generators, DWD removes the decoder and reduces inference cost while maintaining or even improving pixel quality. On Wan video generation, it visibly reduces detail distortion and flickering, lowering warping error by 23%. Remarkably, the same separation also improves pixel diffusion models that do not even use a decoder: a final-step DWD head produces substantially better texture detail in text-to-image generation, and improves ImageNet FID from 1.55 to 1.43, a new state of the art among pixel flow models."

Abstract A diagnostic benchmark and two-phase reinforcement learning framework enable personalized vision-language assistants to jointly connect visual hazard evidence with personal memory for appropriate responses.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Personalized vision-language assistants use visual observations and personal memories to provide advice tailored to the user. When the observed scene contains a physical hazard, personal context, such as allergies, medical conditions, or mobility constraints, can change what help is appropriate. Responding appropriately therefore requires recognizing the visible hazard and applying the relevant person's information. We introduce PReMind-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark that evaluates this combination across diverse hazards and personal-memory conditions. It separately measures memory access, grounding, abstention, and hazard response, and assesses joint success on interdependency cases. Existing models often satisfy one requirement while failing the other, and training only for safety or personalization leaves a gap in joint success. We therefore propose PReMind, a two-phase reinforcement learning framework that combines on-demand and forced-call interactions (memory always retrieved) to jointly train grounded memory use and hazard response. At 4B, PReMind raises joint success from 12.9% to 28.6% over its base on the interdependency set and improves hazard adequacy and correct abstention on the main test. Joint-success gains persist under forced-call evaluation and under a second LLM judge across three Phase II seeds; averaged over these seeds, joint success also improves on unseen hazard groups. These findings highlight the need to evaluate and train personal assistants to connect visual evidence with the relevant person's context.

Бенчмарк раздельно измеряет доступ к памяти, grounding, воздержание от ответа и реакцию на опасность, а также совместный успех на случаях взаимозависимости. Авторы предлагают двухфазный фреймворк reinforcement learning PReMind, который сочетает режимы on-demand и forced-call для совместного обучения использованию памяти и реакции на опасность. Abstract A new benchmark and agentic workflows assess vision-language models for automatic video rough-cut, revealing current models struggle to match expert cut plans.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Video rough-cut is a core stage in media production workflows, organizing raw media materials into an initial video cut. Despite recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) for video-related tasks, automatic rough-cut remains underexplored, without a comprehensive benchmark for evaluation or mature workflows for producing executable cut plans. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce RoughCut-Bench, which covers two practical settings: (1) a visually driven setting over video cases, covering highlight detection and selection, source-video ordering, and adherence to user requirements when provided; and (2) a speech-driven setting over automatic speech recognition (ASR) transcripts, covering filler removal, expression deduplication, and low-value content trimming. Across these settings, the benchmark contains 101 visually driven cases, including 831 raw videos with 1,476 highlight annotations. It also includes 84 speech-driven cases, each consisting of an ASR transcript with annotated cleanup spans. The evaluation compares cut plans with these annotations in both settings and additionally uses a human-aligned agentic evaluator to assess rendered-cut quality in the visual setting. To apply LVLMs to automatic rough-cut, we further propose agentic workflows that guide them to produce structured cut plans from input materials. Experiments show that current LVLMs remain limited in producing expert-aligned cut plans. Our benchmark, code, and prompts are released at https://whalesong-zrs.github.io/RoughCut-Bench-Project-Page.
[19.11.2026 11:09] Response: {"desc": "В статье представлен новый бенчмарк RoughCut-Bench для оценки больших vision-language моделей (LVLM) в задаче автоматического rough-cut, то есть первичной сборки видео из сырого медиаматериала. Бенчмарк включает два режима: визуальный, где модель выбирает хайлайты, упорядочивает исходные видео и учитывает пользовательские требования, и речевой, где по ASR-транскриптам нужно удалять слова-паразиты, дубликаты и малоценные фрагменты. Авторы также предлагают агентные workflow, которые пошагово направляют LVLM к созданию структурированных план-монтажа, а качество итогового ролика оценивается агентным evaluator, согласованным с человеческими оценками. "Abstract A new benchmark and agentic workflows assess vision-language models for automatic video rough-cut, revealing current models struggle to match expert cut plans.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Video rough-cut is a core stage in media production workflows, organizing raw media materials into an initial video cut. Despite recent advances in large vision-language models (LVLMs) for video-related tasks, automatic rough-cut remains underexplored, without a comprehensive benchmark for evaluation or mature workflows for producing executable cut plans. To address the evaluation gap, we introduce RoughCut-Bench, which covers two practical settings: (1) a visually driven setting over video cases, covering highlight detection and selection, source-video ordering, and adherence to user requirements when provided; and (2) a speech-driven setting over automatic speech recognition (ASR) transcripts, covering filler removal, expression deduplication, and low-value content trimming. Across these settings, the benchmark contains 101 visually driven cases, including 831 raw videos with 1,476 highlight annotations. It also includes 84 speech-driven cases, each consisting of an ASR transcript with annotated cleanup spans. The evaluation compares cut plans with these annotations in both settings and additionally uses a human-aligned agentic evaluator to assess rendered-cut quality in the visual setting. To apply LVLMs to automatic rough-cut, we further propose agentic workflows that guide them to produce structured cut plans from input materials. Experiments show that current LVLMs remain limited in producing expert-aligned cut plans. Our benchmark, code, and prompts are released at https://whalesong-zrs.github.io/RoughCut-Bench-Project-Page."

Abstract TIDEWAY enables efficient inference of large Mixture-of-Experts models on memory-constrained consumer devices by treating host RAM and GPU VRAM as a unified memory pool with phase-aware layouts and optimized CUDA Graph execution under expert paging.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash Large language model (LLM) agents are rapidly becoming a dominant paradigm for AI applications. Recently, there is a growing demand to deploy large-scale Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models locally on consumer PCs to balance data privacy, API costs, and inference efficiency. However, unlike datacenter servers with abundant DRAM, consumer devices are tightly constrained in both GPU VRAM and host RAM, forcing massive MoE weights to spill across VRAM, host RAM, and SSD. Under such spilling, we observe that existing systems fail to fully utilize this memory hierarchy, treating the tiers as independent caches and thrashing in inference.   We present TIDEWAY, a system for efficient inference of large MoE models on memory-constrained consumer devices. Our core insight is to manage host RAM and GPU VRAM as a unified memory pool. Within this pool, TIDEWAY couples phase-aware memory layouts, which adapt to the distinct access patterns of prefill and decode, with an execution engine that optimizes CUDA Graph execution under expert paging. Our evaluations on representative consumer hardware with frontier MoE models demonstrate that TIDEWAY significantly outperforms state-of-the-art systems. On average, TIDEWAY achieves speedups of 1.97x in end-to-end latency and 2.40x in Time-to-First-Token (TTFT), along with 1.61x higher decode throughput against the fastest baseline.   Our source code is publicly available at https://github.com/Afterglow231/Tideway .
[19.11.2026 11:09] Response: {"desc": "TIDEWAY — это система для эффективного инференса больших MoE-моделей (Mixture-of-Experts) на потребительских ПК с ограниченной памятью. Ключевая идея заключается в том, чтобы рассматривать оперативную память хоста (host RAM) и видеопамять GPU (VRAM) как единый пул памяти с учётом фаз prefill и decode. Система сочетает такие раскладки памяти с оптимизированным выполнением CUDA Graph при expert paging, что уменьшает трэшинг между уровнями памяти. По результатам экспериментов TIDEWAY ускоряет end-to-end задержку в 1.97 раза, TTFT в 2.40 раза и пропускную способность декодирования в 1.61 раза относительно лучших базовых решений.", "emoji": "🚀", "title": "Единая память для больших MoE-моделей на домашнем ПК"}

Abstract  We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.

Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI."

Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract  Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.
[19.11.2026 11:09] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает стратегию «позднего входа» для циклических языковых моделей (looped LLM), при которой новые токены пропускают первые проходы и сразу попадают в последние K из T проходов над глубоким KV-кэшем контекста. После лёгкого дообучения, устраняющего рассогласование норм скрытых состояний на границе входа, такой подход превосходит классический early exit при том же бюджете проходов. Метод Wake позволяет совмещать точное greedy-декодирование (tau = 0), декодирование с проверкой по порогу логит-маржи (tau > 0) и lossy-вариант без отката (tau = inf) в одном статическом CUDA graph. Abstract FVSCORE is a neuro-symbolic evaluation framework that compiles videos into prompt and implicit constraint propositions, verifies them with perceptual tools and soft temporal logic, and calibrates significant violations to produce interpretable, human-aligned feedback for video generation.  Generated by deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash We introduce Faithfulness Video Score (FVSCORE), a neuro-symbolic framework for specification-driven video generation evaluation. Existing evaluators rely on holistic MLLM judgments or vision-language metrics, yet aligning their scores with human judgments and tracing them to specific temporal violations remain open challenges. FVSCORE addresses these limitations by compiling each video into explicit propositions capturing prompt requirements and implicit propositions capturing unstated physical, temporal, and cross-modal constraints. It grounds each proposition using specialized perceptual tools and evaluates the resulting confidence traces with soft temporal operators inspired by Linear Temporal Logic. A Significance-Aware Calibration step then aligns verification with human perception by retaining only perceptually significant violations before producing a timestamp- and evidence-grounded report. We conduct extensive experiments across public and complex multi-event benchmarks with the strongest video models to date, demonstrating that FVSCORE outperforms holistic and agent-based evaluators in human correlation while providing highly accurate and interpretable feedback.

Existing evaluators rely on holistic MLLM judgments or vision-language metrics, yet aligning their scores with human judgments and tracing them to specific temporal violations remain open challenges. Abstract  Adapting a film into a game requires selecting source elements and translating them into player actions, rules, and feedback. We introduce Movie2Game-Bench (M2G-bench), a benchmark for evaluating film-to-game adaptation beyond playability. M2G-bench combines general evaluation, movie-specific evaluation, and playtesting by three GUI model players. The protocol connects source content to gameplay steps and implementation evidence, capturing both content preservation and interactive realization. The benchmark comprises 22 films and 658 source-specific reference items.
